Yes… yes, I do get days like that. I feel almost radiant, like I’ve been chosen for something important, like everything is lining up and I can finally see what needs to happen.

Not just annoyed, furious. It feels like they’re trying to erase me, or erase what I’m supposed to do. People always say “be practical,” but practical is how they keep everything in little boxes, you know? The boxes don’t organize themselves, and the stories won’t just climb onto the walls politely and ask to be seen.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued for at least two weeks during a time when your mood was not especially high or energized?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that did happen. There were a few weeks where I wasn’t in that bright, driven state. Yes. I still believed it. I mean, I still do, really. The museum board and the city arts people have been monitoring my phone because they know my Caribbean archive would change how the galleries tell the truth.

And sometimes it happens in empty rooms at the museum too, which is strange because those rooms echo anyway, you know, especially near photographs—old photographs have a way of feeling like they’re looking back at you.\n\nBut it’s like women whispering from behind the walls, telling me which photographs belong in the archive. I mean… I don’t think it’s random. I’m pretty sure the museum board and those city arts people are involved somehow. They’ve been monitoring my phone—I know that sounds like a lot, but they know my Caribbean archive would change how the galleries tell the truth, and people get very nervous when the labels on the wall have to be rewritten.\n\nAnd no one has shown me anything that proves they’re not doing it. They just act polite, like at those openings with the little crackers, and meanwhile they know.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start hearing these voices?\n\nPatient: I think… about eight months now. I can’t really pin it down that neatly. I’ve had sad periods, empty ones too, where the house feels too quiet and even making toast seems like some ridiculous chore.

Yes… yes, I think there was. It wasn’t one of those times where I was rushing around with big plans or feeling like I’d discovered some important pattern in everything. I wasn’t especially up like that, and I don’t think I was sunk down in bed either.

But the whispers were still there in the apartment. Especially when it got quiet—though apartments are never really quiet, are they, with pipes and people upstairs and that little hum from chargers. And I still felt sure the arts people were monitoring my phone. Not just a passing worry. It stayed for several weeks, at least. I remember trying not to touch the phone too much, which is silly because then you need it for ordinary things, like checking when the museum opens, or calling about a bill… but yes, it was still there even when my mood wasn’t especially high or low.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF25.0, F20.0, F22, F23.0, F06.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came because I keep hearing these voices, clear voices, in my apartment. And sometimes it happens in empty rooms at the museum too, which is strange because those rooms echo anyway, you know, especially near photographs—old photographs have a way of feeling like they’re looking back at you.\n\nBut it’s like women whispering from behind the walls, telling me which photographs belong in the archive. And I know that sounds… I don’t know. It’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe someone or something is deliberately sending you these voices?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t think it’s random. I’m pretty sure the museum board and those city arts people are involved somehow. They’ve been monitoring my phone—I know that sounds like a lot, but they know my Caribbean archive would change how the galleries tell the truth, and people get very nervous when the labels on the wall have to be rewritten.\n\nAnd no one has shown me anything that proves they’re not doing it. They just act polite, like at those openings with the little crackers, and meanwhile they know.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start hearing these voices?\n\nPatient: I think… about eight months now. It’s hard to pin down because at first I thought it was just the building pipes or neighbors, and the museum has those strange old walls, you know, they carry sound in odd ways.\n\nBut the voices, and that feeling—well, more than a feeling—that people have been watching my archive, paying attention to what I’m collecting… that’s been going on for around eight months.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, have you had a period when you felt unusually high, energized, or elated for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do get days like that. I feel almost radiant, like I’ve been chosen for something important, like everything is lining up and I can finally see what needs to happen.\n\nAnd then if somebody questions me—if they say, “slow down,” or act like my plan is too much—I get furious. Not just annoyed, furious. It feels like they’re trying to erase me, or erase what I’m supposed to do. People always say “be practical,” but practical is how they keep everything in little boxes, you know? Like the labels at the museum, all neat and wrong.\n\nDoctor: During those radiant, energized days, do you need much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I don’t really think of it as “needing” sleep then. I can sleep two, maybe three hours, and then I’m up before dawn like someone switched the lights on inside me.\n\nAnd honestly, I’ll tell myself, sleep is for people who don’t have an archive to rescue. Because if I don’t do it, who will? The boxes don’t organize themselves, and the stories won’t just climb onto the walls politely and ask to be seen.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued for at least two weeks during a time when your mood was not especially high or energized?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that did happen. There were a few weeks where I wasn’t in that bright, driven state. I wasn’t staying up all night or feeling like I had some grand calling, not in the same way.\n\nBut I still heard the whispers in the apartment. Little bits, like through the walls or near the phone, and I still felt—well, I knew—the arts people were monitoring my phone. It’s funny, because everyone thinks arts people are gentle, but committees can be very quiet and very sharp. So yes, even when my mood was more… ordinary, it didn’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Before these voices started, had you been diagnosed with any brain or neurological condition?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think anyone told me I had something like that. Brain, neurological—those are big words, aren’t they? They make me think of scans and wires and those awful waiting rooms with old magazines.\n\nBefore the voices, I mean, I can’t remember being diagnosed with anything like that. Not that anyone explained to me, anyway. Sometimes doctors say things in a hallway tone, like you’re supposed to just absorb it, but no… I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to measure that exactly. Two weeks sounds very official, like something you’d mark on a calendar, and I don’t always keep track that way.\n\nI’ve had low stretches, yes, where the day feels heavy and I don’t want to do much, but whether it was most of the day nearly every day for two whole weeks… I don’t know. Sometimes the mornings are worse, sometimes the afternoons sort of disappear. It all runs together a bit.\n\nDoctor: During the times when the voices continued but your mood felt ordinary, did you still believe the museum board and arts people were monitoring your phone?\n\nPatient: Yes. I still believed it. I mean, I still do, really. The museum board and the city arts people have been monitoring my phone because they know my Caribbean archive would change how the galleries tell the truth.\n\nAnd people act like that sounds dramatic, but museums are full of little decisions—labels, lighting, whose grandmother’s story gets put behind glass and whose gets left in a box. I spent years thinking about those things. No one has shown me anything that proves they weren’t listening or watching. So yes, even when my mood felt ordinary, that didn’t go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt depressed nearly all day, every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know if I can say it was nearly all day, every day. I’ve had times where I felt very low, yes, like everything was gray around the edges, but then I’d get distracted by something—tea boiling over, a bird at the window, some old paper I meant to sort—and I don’t know if that counts as it letting up or just me moving around it.\n\nIt’s hard to put it in that exact box. I remember heavy days, but not cleanly enough to say, “yes, every day, all day,” like a schedule.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever gone through a period of at least two weeks when you felt sad or empty most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I can’t really pin it down that neatly. I mean… I don’t think it’s random. I’m pretty sure the museum board and those city arts people are involved somehow. I feel almost radiant, like I’ve been chosen for something important, like everything is lining up and I can finally see what needs to happen.\n\nAnd then if somebody questions me—if they say, “slow down,” or act like my plan is too much—I get furious. I mean, I don’t really think of it as “needing” sleep then. The boxes don’t organize themselves, and the stories won’t just climb onto the walls politely and ask to be seen.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued for at least two weeks during a time when your mood was not especially high or energized?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that did happen. There were a few weeks where I wasn’t in that bright, driven state. Brain, neurological—those are big words, aren’t they? I wasn’t staying up all night or feeling like I had some grand calling, not in the same way.\n\nBut I still heard the whispers in the apartment. Little bits, like through the walls or near the phone, and I still felt—well, I knew—the arts people were monitoring my phone. It’s funny, because everyone thinks arts people are gentle, but committees can be very quiet and very sharp. So yes, even when my mood was more… ordinary, it didn’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Before these voices started, had you been diagnosed with any brain or neurological condition?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think anyone told me I had something like that. Brain, neurological—those are big words, aren’t they? They make me think of scans and wires and those awful waiting rooms with old magazines.\n\nBefore the voices, I mean, I can’t remember being diagnosed with anything like that. Not that anyone explained to me, anyway. Sometimes doctors say things in a hallway tone, like you’re supposed to just absorb it, but no… I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to measure that exactly. Two weeks sounds very official, like something you’d mark on a calendar, and I don’t always keep track that way.\n\nI’ve had low stretches, yes, where the day feels heavy and I don’t want to do much, but whether it was most of the day nearly every day for two whole weeks… I don’t know. Sometimes the mornings are worse, sometimes the afternoons sort of disappear. It all runs together a bit.\n\nDoctor: During the times when the voices continued but your mood felt ordinary, did you still believe the museum board and arts people were monitoring your phone?\n\nPatient: Yes. I still believed it. I mean, I still do, really. The museum board and the city arts people have been monitoring my phone because they know my Caribbean archive would change how the galleries tell the truth.\n\nAnd people act like that sounds dramatic, but museums are full of little decisions—labels, lighting, whose grandmother’s story gets put behind glass and whose gets left in a box. I spent years thinking about those things. No one has shown me anything that proves they weren’t listening or watching. So yes, even when my mood felt ordinary, that didn’t go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt depressed nearly all day, every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know if I can say it was nearly all day, every day. I’ve had times where I felt very low, yes, like everything was gray around the edges, but then I’d get distracted by something—tea boiling over, a bird at the window, some old paper I meant to sort—and I don’t know if that counts as it letting up or just me moving around it.\n\nIt’s hard to put it in that exact box. I remember heavy days, but not cleanly enough to say, “yes, every day, all day,” like a schedule.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever gone through a period of at least two weeks when you felt sad or empty most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I can’t really pin it down that neatly. I’ve had sad periods, empty ones too, where the house feels too quiet and even making toast seems like some ridiculous chore.\n\nBut at least two weeks, most of the day, nearly every day… I don’t know. Time gets slippery when you’re in it. I’ll think, “this has been going on forever,” and then I realize I’m remembering three different winters mixed together, or the week the neighbor’s tree came down, or something I read in an old exhibition catalogue. So I can’t honestly say yes to that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices begin suddenly, over just a few days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it was suddenly like that. A few days sounds so tidy, like turning on a radio and there it is. I remember noticing things, but memory doesn’t line itself up politely, you know?\n\nSometimes I think it crept in, and then other times I think maybe I only noticed it all at once because I was tired or because the house was too quiet. It’s hard for me to separate the beginning from when I finally paid attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Was there a full month when you were not especially high, energized, or depressed, but the voices or the belief that people were monitoring your phone were still there?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think there was. It wasn’t one of those times where I was rushing around with big plans or feeling like I’d discovered some important pattern in everything. I wasn’t especially up like that, and I don’t think I was sunk down in bed either.\n\nBut the whispers were still there in the apartment. Especially when it got quiet—though apartments are never really quiet, are they, with pipes and people upstairs and that little hum from chargers. And I still felt sure the arts people were monitoring my phone. Not just a passing worry. It stayed for several weeks, at least. I remember trying not to touch the phone too much, which is silly because then you need it for ordinary things, like checking when the museum opens, or calling about a bill… but yes, it was still there even when my mood wasn’t especially high or low.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices or your belief that people were monitoring your phone lasted for at least one month?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more than a month. It’s been about eight months now, I think.\n\nThe voices, and that certainty that people are watching my archive—my papers, the things I’ve kept, the clippings and notes, not that anyone ever understands why those matter—but yes, it’s been there for months. Since… well, I start counting and then I get distracted because there were boxes I meant to sort in the dining room, and one of them had old gallery postcards in it, and that’s around when I remember it being already there.\n\nSo yes. I don’t know if I can measure it that neatly. Two weeks sounds like something you could mark off on a calendar, but I’m not sure I was keeping track in that way.

Yes… I think so. Most of these months have been more like that—driven, like I had to keep going, had to follow through on things, like there was some purpose to it. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came because I keep hearing these voices, clear voices, in my apartment. And sometimes it happens in empty rooms at the museum too, which is strange because those rooms echo anyway, you know, especially near photographs—old photographs have a way of feeling like they’re looking back at you.\n\nBut it’s like women whispering from behind the walls, telling me which photographs belong in the archive. And I know that sounds… I don’t know. It’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe someone or something is deliberately sending you these voices?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t think it’s random. I’m pretty sure the museum board and those city arts people are involved somehow. They’ve been monitoring my phone—I know that sounds like a lot, but they know my Caribbean archive would change how the galleries tell the truth, and people get very nervous when the labels on the wall have to be rewritten.\n\nAnd no one has shown me anything that proves they’re not doing it. They just act polite, like at those openings with the little crackers, and meanwhile they know.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start hearing these voices?\n\nPatient: I think… about eight months now. It’s hard to pin down because at first I thought it was just the building pipes or neighbors, and the museum has those strange old walls, you know, they carry sound in odd ways.\n\nBut the voices, and that feeling—well, more than a feeling—that people have been watching my archive, paying attention to what I’m collecting… that’s been going on for around eight months.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, have you had a period when you felt unusually high, energized, or elated for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do get days like that. I feel almost radiant, like I’ve been chosen for something important, like everything is lining up and I can finally see what needs to happen.\n\nAnd then if somebody questions me—if they say, “slow down,” or act like my plan is too much—I get furious. Not just annoyed, furious. It feels like they’re trying to erase me, or erase what I’m supposed to do. People always say “be practical,” but practical is how they keep everything in little boxes, you know? Like the labels at the museum, all neat and wrong.\n\nDoctor: During those radiant, energized days, do you need much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I don’t really think of it as “needing” sleep then. I can sleep two, maybe three hours, and then I’m up before dawn like someone switched the lights on inside me.\n\nAnd honestly, I’ll tell myself, sleep is for people who don’t have an archive to rescue. Because if I don’t do it, who will? The boxes don’t organize themselves, and the stories won’t just climb onto the walls politely and ask to be seen.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued for at least two weeks during a time when your mood was not especially high or energized?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that did happen. There were a few weeks where I wasn’t in that bright, driven state. I wasn’t staying up all night or feeling like I had some grand calling, not in the same way.\n\nBut I still heard the whispers in the apartment. Little bits, like through the walls or near the phone, and I still felt—well, I knew—the arts people were monitoring my phone. It’s funny, because everyone thinks arts people are gentle, but committees can be very quiet and very sharp. So yes, even when my mood was more… ordinary, it didn’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Before these voices started, had you been diagnosed with any brain or neurological condition?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think anyone told me I had something like that. Brain, neurological—those are big words, aren’t they? They make me think of scans and wires and those awful waiting rooms with old magazines.\n\nBefore the voices, I mean, I can’t remember being diagnosed with anything like that. Not that anyone explained to me, anyway. Sometimes doctors say things in a hallway tone, like you’re supposed to just absorb it, but no… I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to measure that exactly. Two weeks sounds very official, like something you’d mark on a calendar, and I don’t always keep track that way.\n\nI’ve had low stretches, yes, where the day feels heavy and I don’t want to do much, but whether it was most of the day nearly every day for two whole weeks… I don’t know. Sometimes the mornings are worse, sometimes the afternoons sort of disappear. It all runs together a bit.\n\nDoctor: During the times when the voices continued but your mood felt ordinary, did you still believe the museum board and arts people were monitoring your phone?\n\nPatient: Yes. I still believed it. I mean, I still do, really. The museum board and the city arts people have been monitoring my phone because they know my Caribbean archive would change how the galleries tell the truth.\n\nAnd people act like that sounds dramatic, but museums are full of little decisions—labels, lighting, whose grandmother’s story gets put behind glass and whose gets left in a box. I spent years thinking about those things. No one has shown me anything that proves they weren’t listening or watching. So yes, even when my mood felt ordinary, that didn’t go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt depressed nearly all day, every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know if I can say it was nearly all day, every day. I’ve had times where I felt very low, yes, like everything was gray around the edges, but then I’d get distracted by something—tea boiling over, a bird at the window, some old paper I meant to sort—and I don’t know if that counts as it letting up or just me moving around it.\n\nIt’s hard to put it in that exact box. I remember heavy days, but not cleanly enough to say, “yes, every day, all day,” like a schedule.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever gone through a period of at least two weeks when you felt sad or empty most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I can’t really pin it down that neatly. I’ve had sad periods, empty ones too, where the house feels too quiet and even making toast seems like some ridiculous chore.\n\nBut at least two weeks, most of the day, nearly every day… I don’t know. Time gets slippery when you’re in it. I’ll think, “this has been going on forever,” and then I realize I’m remembering three different winters mixed together, or the week the neighbor’s tree came down, or something I read in an old exhibition catalogue. So I can’t honestly say yes to that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices begin suddenly, over just a few days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it was suddenly like that. A few days sounds so tidy, like turning on a radio and there it is. I remember noticing things, but memory doesn’t line itself up politely, you know?\n\nSometimes I think it crept in, and then other times I think maybe I only noticed it all at once because I was tired or because the house was too quiet. It’s hard for me to separate the beginning from when I finally paid attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Was there a full month when you were not especially high, energized, or depressed, but the voices or the belief that people were monitoring your phone were still there?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think there was. It wasn’t one of those times where I was rushing around with big plans or feeling like I’d discovered some important pattern in everything. I wasn’t especially up like that, and I don’t think I was sunk down in bed either.\n\nBut the whispers were still there in the apartment. Especially when it got quiet—though apartments are never really quiet, are they, with pipes and people upstairs and that little hum from chargers. And I still felt sure the arts people were monitoring my phone. Not just a passing worry. It stayed for several weeks, at least. I remember trying not to touch the phone too much, which is silly because then you need it for ordinary things, like checking when the museum opens, or calling about a bill… but yes, it was still there even when my mood wasn’t especially high or low.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices or your belief that people were monitoring your phone lasted for at least one month?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more than a month. It’s been about eight months now, I think.\n\nThe voices, and that certainty that people are watching my archive—my papers, the things I’ve kept, the clippings and notes, not that anyone ever understands why those matter—but yes, it’s been there for months. Since… well, I start counting and then I get distracted because there were boxes I meant to sort in the dining room, and one of them had old gallery postcards in it, and that’s around when I remember it being already there.\n\nSo yes. Around eight months.\n\nDoctor: During the past eight months, was there ever a time when you felt depressed nearly all day, almost every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can measure it that neatly. Two weeks sounds like something you could mark off on a calendar, but I’m not sure I was keeping track in that way.\n\nThere were days I felt low, yes, and some mornings I didn’t want to get started, but then I’d get distracted by the mail piling up or something on the kitchen counter that needed sorting. And then I’d think about how the light comes in differently in the afternoon than it used to—though that may just be the season.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I can’t say for certain it was nearly all day, almost every day for two weeks.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, did the high, energized periods make up most of that time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Most of these months have been more like that—driven, like I had to keep going, had to follow through on things, like there was some purpose to it. Since retirement it’s strange, because people think you’re supposed to slow down, have tea, join a little class, but I felt more… chosen, almost, and then irritated when anyone interrupted or acted like I should just relax.\n\nThere were quieter days too, days when I withdrew and didn’t want to deal with anyone. But if you’re asking what took up most of the time, I’d say the high, energized stretches did. Like I couldn’t really slow myself down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
